import pandas as pd
import numpy as np


def global_preprocessing(X_data, leak_labels, time_start, time_win_len, sensors_idx=None):
    X = []
    Y = []
    y_leak = []
    
    if sensors_idx is None:
        sensors_idx = list(range(X_data.shape[1]))
    
     # Use a sliding time window to construct a labeled data set
    t_index = time_start
    time_points = range(len(leak_labels))
    i = 0
    while t_index < len(time_points) - time_win_len:
        # Grab time window from data stream
        x = X_data[t_index:t_index+time_win_len-1, sensors_idx]

        #######################
        # Feature engineering #
        #######################
        x = np.mean(x,axis=0)  # "Stupid" feature
        X.append(x)

        Y.append([X_data[t_index + time_win_len-1, n] for n in sensors_idx])

        y_leak.append(leak_labels[t_index + time_win_len-1])

        t_index += 1  # Note: Overlapping time windows
        i += 1

    X = np.array(X)
    Y = np.array(Y)
    y_leak = np.array(y_leak)

    return X, Y, y_leak

import os

path_to_data = "data/"

time_win_len = 4  # Always consider the last four time steps
time_start = 100  # Ignore the first 100 samples

t_train_split = 3000    # Use the first 3000 sampels for training -- there are no anomalies present early until end of January!  
batch_size = 200  # Number of samples used for adapting the model to the fault


def load_data(scenario_id, use_faulty_sensor_scenario=False):
    # Open and read file
    df_pressures = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/Results/Measurements_Pressures.csv"))
    df_flows = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/Results/Measurements_Flows.csv"))
    
    # Parse labels
    df_labels = df_pressures[["Timestamp"]].copy()
    df_labels["label"] = 0

    def parse_leak_data(): # TODO: Assuming there is only one leaky node!
        leaky_pipe, start, end = None, None, None
        
        # Locate and load file with information on the leak
        leak_info_file = list(filter(lambda z: z.endswith(".xlsx"), os.listdir(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsLeakages/"))))[0]
        df_leak_info = pd.read_excel(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsLeakages/{leak_info_file}"), sheet_name="Info", engine='openpyxl')
        
        # Parse for start and end time of leak
        for _, row in df_leak_info.iterrows():
            if row["Description"] == "Leak Start":
                start = row["Value"]
            elif row["Description"] == "Leak End":
                end = row["Value"]
            elif row["Description"] == "Leak Pipe":
                leaky_pipe = row["Value"]
        
        return leaky_pipe, start, end

    leaky_pipe, start, end = parse_leak_data()

    # Load faulty sensor information
    def load_faulty_sensor_info():
        faulty_sensor_id, start, end = None, None, None

        faulty_sensor_info_file = list(filter(lambda z: z.endswith(".csv"), os.listdir(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/WithoutSensorFaults/"))))[0]
        df_faulty_sensor_info = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/WithoutSensorFaults/{faulty_sensor_info_file}"))
        for _, row in df_faulty_sensor_info.iterrows():
            if row["Description"] == "Sensor":
                faulty_sensor_id = row["Value"].replace("node", "") # Remove naming artificat from the generator
            elif row["Description"] == "Fault Start":
                start = row["Value"]
            elif row["Description"] == "Fault End":
                end = row["Value"]

        return faulty_sensor_id, start, end
    
    faulty_sensor_id = None
    if use_faulty_sensor_scenario is True:
        faulty_sensor_id, start, end = load_faulty_sensor_info()    # Note: We override start and end time of the leakage

    if use_faulty_sensor_scenario is True:  # Data from which simulation should be used?
        df_pressures = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/Measurements_Pressures.csv"))
        df_flows = pd.read_csv(os.path.join(path_to_data, f"Scenario-{scenario_id}/ResultsSensorFaults/Measurements_Flows.csv"))

    # Parse labels
    indices = df_labels[(df_labels["Timestamp"] >= start) & (df_labels["Timestamp"] <= end)].index
    for idx in indices:
        df_labels["label"].loc[idx] = 1

    labels = df_labels["label"].to_numpy().flatten()

    # Parse pressure and flow measurements
    df_pressures.drop(columns=["Timestamp"], inplace=True)
    df_flows.drop(columns=["Timestamp"], inplace=True)

    pressure_nodes = list(df_pressures.columns)
    flow_nodes = list(df_flows.columns)
    nodes = pressure_nodes + flow_nodes

    pressures_per_node = {}
    for node_id in pressure_nodes:
        pressures_per_node[node_id] = df_pressures[[node_id]].to_numpy().flatten()

    flows_per_node = {}
    for node_id in flow_nodes:
        flows_per_node[node_id] = df_flows[[node_id]].to_numpy().flatten()

    # Build numpy arrays
    y = labels
    X = np.vstack([pressures_per_node[n] for n in pressure_nodes] + [flows_per_node[n] for n in flow_nodes]).T

    return X, y, nodes, pressure_nodes, flow_nodes, leaky_pipe, faulty_sensor_id


def score(Y_True, Y_pred):
    Y_pred = Y_pred.reshape(Y_True.shape)
    return [mean_squared_error(Y_True[:, i], Y_pred[:, i]) for i in range(Y_True.shape[1])]

6.4. Baseline Comparison on Benchmark WDNs
In this set of experiments we compared the performance of GATRes‐small against two state‐of‐the‐art baseline
models, GraphConvWat (Hajgató et al., 2021a, 2021b) and mGCN (Ashraf et al., 2023). We evaluated the three
models on four benchmark WDNs: Anytown, C‐Town, Richmond, and L‐Town, described in Section 5.1. The
experiments were conducted following the method proposed by Hajgató
et al. (2020) to ensure a fair comparison. Specifically, 1,000, 10,000, and
20,000 snapshots were generated for the C‐Town, L‐Town, and Richmond
WDNs, respectively. Then, the data sets were split into training, validation,
and test sets in a 6:2:2 ratio.
We used the same experimental settings as proposed in the baseline ap-
proaches to guarantee a fair comparison. Thus, in all experiments the models
are trained for 2,000 epochs with early stopping, using the Adam gradient‐
based optimization algorithm (Kingma & Ba, 2014). The GraphConvWat
model training was stopped if the validation loss did not improve for 50
consecutive epochs. In the case of mGCN, the training was stopped if no
improvement is seen after 250 epochs. Likewise mGCN, our model training is
stopped after 250 epochs if no improvement is observed. In all cases, it is
considered an improvement when the validation loss decreases at least
by 1e−6.

import pandas as pd
import numpy as np


def global_preprocessing(X_data, leak_labels, time_start, time_win_len, sensors_idx=None):
    X = []
    Y = []
    y_leak = []
    
    if sensors_idx is None:
        sensors_idx = list(range(X_data.shape[1]))
    
     # Use a sliding time window to construct a labeled data set
    t_index = time_start
    time_points = range(len(leak_labels))
    i = 0
    while t_index < len(time_points) - time_win_len:
        # Grab time window from data stream
        x = X_data[t_index:t_index+time_win_len-1, sensors_idx]

        #######################
        # Feature engineering #
        #######################
        x = np.mean(x,axis=0)  # "Stupid" feature
        X.append(x)

        Y.append([X_data[t_index + time_win_len-1, n] for n in sensors_idx])

        y_leak.append(leak_labels[t_index + time_win_len-1])

        t_index += 1  # Note: Overlapping time windows
        i += 1

    X = np.array(X)
    Y = np.array(Y)
    y_leak = np.array(y_leak)

    return X, Y, y_leak

154:        X_batch, Y_batch = X_all_test[t:t+batch_size,:], Y_all_test[t:t+batch_size,:]
156:        # Apply anomaly detection to current batch
157:        time_points, _ = model.apply_detector(X_batch, Y_batch)
161:        # Adapt to current batch
162:        model.fit(X_batch, Y_batch)
164:        # Next batch
165:        t += batch_size
A difference between the simulated pressure data is calculated in
the same way as before for the observed data. Finally, the MSE of
two differences, the observed values’ difference, and the simulated
values’ difference are used as an objective function for this prob-
lem, and we could find a high probability area of leakage with a low
MSE value.
In area C, almost every node has an AMR, facilitating the
accurate building of a hydraulic model in EPANET. We select
one week when the bust had been occurred and compare the
observed and the simulated values to determine the location of
the leakage. Second, we assign the AMR demand data to nodes
and calculate the reservoir head pattern according to the SCADA
data of tank level. Third, search for nodes and emitter coeffi-
cients and find the difference between the observed pressure
value and the simulated value. Finally, we use the same way
to calculate the MSE of the difference and find a high probable
area of leakage.
Fig. 11 shows the result of the leakage that occurred on April 20,
2019, 12:00. The shade in grey in the figure represents the size
of the MSE. The node with darker color has a smaller MSE.
Among them, red, orange, yellow, green, and blue indicate the five
nodes with the smallest MSE, that is, the area with the highest prob-
ability of leakage.
In addition, the performance of the proposed weekly difference
localization method and traditional method are compared in this
section. The distinction between the two methods is whether to
carry out the weekly difference operation. Two metrics are calcu-
lated to evaluate the effectiveness of localization: (1) the distance
between the real leak hotspot and the hotspot found by the
model (m); and (2) the interval number of pipes between the real
leak hotspot and the hotspot found by the model. As shown in
Fig. 12, the trend of the results of the two metrics is basically the
same. Among the 9 leak hotspots in 2018, the proposed localization
method has better positioning accuracy on 7 leak hotspots than tra-
ditional methods. Both methods have the same performance and
accuracy in finding the leak hotspot (3-5). The results of the tradi-
tional method are only slightly better than the proposed methods at
the leak hotspot 1-31. From the perspective of positioning distance,
all of the errors localized by the proposed method are less than
300m, the average localized error is 100.7 m, while the average
error of the traditional method is 385.3m, and the errors of 5 leak
hotspots are greater than 400 m. In addition, the performance of the
traditional method and the proposed weekly difference localization
method for the leakage on October 26, 2018, are shown in Fig. 13.

As the study designs and analytic approaches used in modern research have grown in variability and complexity, it has become more challenging to create a Table 1 that serves to inform readers about issues related to study validity.\n\n## Journal of Clinical Epidemiology\n\nDate: October 2019\n\nPages: 125-132\n\nVolume: Volume 114\n\nView accessibility information\n\n## Published by: Elsevier\n\nResearch article\n\nGet rights and content\n\n# \n\nOriginal Article\n\nWho is in this study, anyway? If well-executed, this “Table 1” can illuminate potential threats to internal and external validity. However, little guidance exists on best practices for designing a Table 1, especially for complex study designs and analyses. We aimed to summarize and extend the literature related to reporting descriptive statistics.\n\n;\n\nTables\n\n;\n\nEpidemiologic methods\n\n;\n\nExternal validity\n\n;\n\nInternal validity\n\n;\n\nGeneralizability\n\n;\n\nClinical research\n\n## Introduction\n\n“Who is in this study?” is the first question many readers of clinical and epidemiologic studies ask. Readers care about who is in a study because it helps them understand and evaluate the study's findings: to assess applicability to other patients or populations (i.e., external validity) and risk of bias (i.e., internal validity) [1], [2]. As a result, papers often include a table that describes the study sample; this is commonly the first table in a paper. This “Table 1,” as it is colloquially called, can be designed to shed light on potential threats to both internal and external validity of study findings.\n\nTable 1 can provide insights on threats to internal validity in the traditional epidemiologic framework: (1) confounding (i.e., differences in other causes of the outcome between exposed and unexposed), (2) selection bias (e.g., differential cohort attrition or control selection), and (3) measurement error [3]. --------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nResearcher's Guide for the Preparation of Tables - ScienceDirect (https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S0022480425001556)\nciteturn85search1 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: 2 months ago; The five steps for the preparation of tables include (1) tables with a clear purpose; (2) using a universal layout; (3) selecting relevant data for Table 1 versus other tables; (4) simplifying variables by categorizing, standardizing, and reducing; and (5) enhancing the readability of numbers and decimals. ... Target journal: Journal of Surgical Research: https://www.sciencedirect.com/journal/journal-of-surgical-research\n\n## Journal of Surgical Research\n\nVolume 310, June 2025, Pages 209-217\n\n# \n\nResearch Methods and Scientific Publishing\n\nResearcher's Guide for the Preparation of Tables\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.jss.2025.03.034Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Abstract\n\nAlmost all scientific articles include tables, but there is little guidance on how to prepare tables. Authors should enhance the focus on the tables’ data-ink, the essential information, and minimizing the no-data-ink, nonessential elements. The six principles of Gestalt on human perception can also be applied to the table to increase readability. Through this review, we provide a practical guide and an overview of how to prepare readable, informative tables. The five steps for the preparation of tables include (1) tables with a clear purpose; (2) using a universal layout; (3) selecting relevant data for Table 1 versus other tables; (4) simplifying variables by categorizing, standardizing, and reducing; and (5) enhancing the readability of numbers and decimals. This results in informative tables that contain data, serving a specific purpose for the reader and increasing readability.\n\n## Keywords\n\nBiomedical research\n\nDocumentation\n\nGuideline\n\nPublishing\n\nTables\n\nTarget journal: Journal of Surgical Research: https://www.sciencedirect.com/journal/journal-of-surgical-research\n\n© 2025 The Author(s). # PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

𝐷 is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.

## 3. Experimental setups

### 3.1. Dataset

Real-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.

Image: Fig 2

  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)
  2. Download: Download full-size image

Fig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.

The l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.

The pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).

### 3.2. Model settings

For the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.

For all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.

### 3.3. Evaluation metric

This study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. The formula is as follows:(13)𝑀⁢𝐴⁢𝐸=1(𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽*⁢𝐼+𝐼*∑𝑖=1𝐽*∑𝑗=1∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗−̃ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∣(14)𝑀⁢𝐴⁢𝑃⁢𝐸=1(𝐼+𝐼*)×𝐽*⁢𝐼+𝐼*∑𝑖=1𝐽*∑𝑗=1∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗−̃ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗∣ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 where ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 is the prediction and ̃ℎ𝑣𝑖,𝑡𝑗 is the simulated measurement of vector 𝑣𝑖 at time step 𝑡𝑗.

Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\\n\\n## 1. Introduction\\n\\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].\\n\\nGenerally, to estimate the water usage at non-monitored sites, a well-calibrated hydraulic model is typically required for indirect observation [4,5,9,15]. The hydraulic model, with their robust theoretical basis and comprehensible parameter settings, serves as the foundation and primary source of information for water network management [8,27,36], and is widely understood and trusted by practitioners. Hydraulic simulation was conducted to enhance the estimation credibility of a real-world network, and demand forecasting can then be obtained by time series forecasting algorithms [23]. However, the uncertainties associated with the water demand and the condition of infrastructure assets make constructing and maintaining a well-calibrated WDS highly complex and time-consuming [1,33]. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.\\n\\nDespite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.\\n\\nThe literature [34] defin"

Dataset\\r\\n\\r\\nReal-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\\r\\n\\r\\nImage: Fig 2\\r\\n\\r\\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\\r\\n  2. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3.2. Model settings\\r\\n\\r\\nFor the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.\\r\\n\\r\\nFor all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.\\r\\n\\r\\n### 3.3. Evaluation metric\\r\\n\\r\\nThis study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect. A review of graph neural networks: concepts, architectures, techniques, challenges, datasets, applications, and future directions\n\nResearch paper\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nAttention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\n\n  * •\n\nModel is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\n\n  * •\n\nModel exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\n\n  * •\n\nModel explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\n\n## Abstract\n\nEfficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\n\n## Graphical abstract\n\nImage: Image, graphical abstract\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (273KB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\n  * Previous article in issue\n  * Next article in issue\n\n## Keywords\n\nExplainability\n\nReal-time\n\nDemand forecasting\n\nAttention mechanisms\n\nGraph neural network\n\nWater distribution system\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. Achieving real-time efficient forecasting based on the hydraulic model seems unattainable, especially for cities lacking a hydraulic model. Consequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.\n\nSeveral studies [18,33,37] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [37] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [33] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. Their research demonstrated the robustness of the GNN model to various levels of noise. By applying the gated graph neural network (GGNN), Li et al. [18] were the first to achieve the state forecasting for water quality. The GGNN algorithm, derived from gated recurrent unit (GRU), was applied for the first time in the domain of the WDS, yielding promising spatiotemporal predictive outputs. Although the aforementioned methods can be used for predicting future water demand states, they still have limitations. The methods in the first two studies were limited to estimating the current state. While integrating time series prediction algorithms with their methods could achieve future state forecasting, the simple stacking of time series forecasting and state estimation would lead to error accumulation. The GGNN method offers a novel approach to developing alternative models for state forecasting in WDSs. However, directly applying the GGNN method does not tightly integrate with the characteristics of water networks. Additionally, GGNN requires recurrent graph convolution operations to expand the receptive field, which limits its application in large-scale pipe networks and leads to over-smoothing issues.\n\nDespite excellent results and a broad range of applications, GNN models are still treated as black boxes [16]: the underlying mechanisms that steer the learning process and generalization ability remain a challenging and open question [29]. Merely achieving good performance is not enough to guarantee the deployment of models in production [13,21], especially in critical operational contexts where additional performance metrics such as robustness or safety are paramount. In such environments, human operators must comprehend and trust the model's predictions. Also, a deeper understanding of the model would improve its predictive performance [22]. Therefore, the second goal of this paper is to explain the results generated by the model.\n\nThis study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms to ensure transparency. The following sections detail the challenges addressed and present the explainable AGN framework.\n\n### 2.1. Problem formulation\n\nBefore discussing the issues and challenges, this study provide a detailed introduction to the symbolic representation of WDS as a graph. The WDS is represented as an undirected graph \\mathcal{G}=\\left(\\mathcal{V},\\mathcal{E},\\mathcal{W}\\right). Here, \\mathcal{V} is the set of I+{I}^{*} vertices representing nodes that comprise reservoirs, tanks and junctions. \\mathcal{E} is the set of edges representing the valves, pumps, and pipes. \\mathcal{W}\\in {\\mathbb{R}}^{\\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)\\times \\left(I+{I}^{*}\\right)} is the weighted adjacency matrix. ## 4. Results and discussion\n\nThis section presents the results from both forecasting performance and model explainability analyses. First, this study report and analyze the forecasting task results: the proposed method achieves superior spatial generalization and temporal forecasting accuracy compared to previous approaches. Subsequently, this study evaluates the robustness of the AGN model across varying prediction windows. Finally, once demonstrated the quality of our predictions, this study explains the recurrent layers, attention mechanisms and nodal features used in the model, demonstrating how these components influence the final output of the model.\n\n### 4.1. Forecasting results\n\n#### 4.1.1. Spatial generalization accuracy\n\nThis study aims to utilize the GNN algorithm to predict the water demand states from a pressure perspective. A thorough review of previous research revealed that, although several pioneering studies have explored the estimation or forecasting of WDS states without relying on pre-built hydraulic models, they still face limitations. Notably, 82.8 % of total nodes have an MAE below 0.5 m, a widely recognized threshold for practical application in hydraulic model calibration. Compared to competing models, AGN shows a significant advantage in the proportion of nodes below this threshold, outperforming GGNN and GHR+GRU methods by 79 % and 73.4 %, respectively, highlighting its superior spatial generalization performance.\n\nFurther analysis of Fig. 3(b) reveals a profound correlation between prediction error and nodal head variation. Nodes with errors between 0.5 m and 1.0 m are mainly concentrated in high-elevation areas, while low-elevation nodes generally face challenges with MAE greater than 2.0 m. This phenomenon indicates an imbalance in model training, particularly as low-elevation nodes are not sufficiently learned due to sparse sensor placement. We attribute this primarily to two factors: (1) sensor scarcity in low-elevation regions hinders the model's ability to capture localized hydraulic characteristics, and (2) insufficient model capacity to discern elevation-driven hydraulic variations. These findings underscore the critical impact of sensor deployment density on data-driven model performance, highlighting the need for comprehensive data acquisition to optimize model training.\n\nImage: Fig 3\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 3. Comparison of forecasting models in spatial direction: (a) The real nodal heads variation; (b) The MAE loss of AGN model; (c) The MAE loss of GGNN model; (d) The MAE loss of GHR+GRU method.\n\nAdmittedly, in the face of complex water network components, drastic elevation changes, and sparse sensor deployment, the AGN model still faces considerable challenges in pursuing comprehensive and accurate predictions. However, it is noteworthy that even under such extreme conditions, the AGN model demonstrates remarkable predictive ability in the high-elevation area, despite the sensors in this area still being relatively sparse — 100 % of high-elevation nodes have an MAE below 1.0 m. In the context of low-density sensor deployment, the AGN can still effectively address sparse sensor environments, reducing reliance on sensing equipment. More real-world WDSs will benefit from our sensor-robust model.\n\nAs shown in Fig. 3(b), the AGN model achieves satisfactory prediction accuracy and breadth in the medium-elevation area. This suggests that in water distribution systems (WDSs) characterized by relatively gentle terrain and moderate head changes, the AGN model can deliver enhanced predictive performance. These results not only further consolidate the practicality and reliability of the AGN model but also lays a solid theoretical and practical foundation for its widespread application in complex WDS management, heralding a promising future for data-driven technology in intelligent WDS management.\n\nDue to their relatively low deployment and maintenance costs, pressure sensors have been widely used in WDSs. This provides a rich data source for the AGN model and practically verifies the applicability and feasibility of water demand prediction from pressure data. This practice-oriented research perspective offers new ideas and methodological support for WDS management.\n\n### 4.2. Results explanation\n\n#### 4.2.1. Rule extraction from recurrent layer\n\nEmploying recurrent graph convolution (as shown in Eq. (5)), the AGN model gradually expands the receptive field of sensor nodes’ neighboring nodes. Simultaneously, pressure feature transfer from sensor nodes to global nodes is achieved by the self-attention mechanism, enabling long-range generalization.\n\nTo understand the rule of recurrent layers and their impact on the model’s generalization ability, this study varied the number of recurrent layers and tested the model’s generalization accuracy under different iterations. Fig. 5(a) shows the relative position relationship between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. The size and color depth of the non-sensor nodes indicate their distance from the nearest sensor nodes. Additionally, the data statistics in the upper right corner reveal the shortest path distribution between sensor nodes and non-sensor nodes. Notably, most non-sensor nodes are three steps away from sensor nodes, with the longest path being eleven steps. This is primarily due to the attention mechanism’s ability to capture long-distance correlations, allowing nodes to transcend physical distance limitations. Through the self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\n\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. Intuitively, sensor nodes in central areas (e.g., 458, 549, and 752) enjoy higher weights due to their critical positions, significantly enhancing the overall prediction performance. In contrast, sensors at the network periphery (e.g., nodes 114, 469, and 769) are less important. The concepts of geographical center and periphery do not exist in graph theory; only the concept of connectivity does. In other words, nodes that exhibit better connectivity or play a crucial role in the network’s connectivity were trained with higher weights. Placing sensor nodes at critical connectivity points appears to be key to improving the performance of graph-based data-driven models. The importance related to node connectivity underscores the need to optimize sensor layouts, especially in selecting critical connections. However, further cross-network studies are needed to definitively establish the correlation between sensor placement and connectivity.\n\nBy introducing the self-attention mechanism into the gating mechanism, this study broadens the receptive field of sensor nodes and effectively captures global information. Additionally, it deepens our understanding of the working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. These findings provide theoretical support for optimizing existing models and lay a solid foundation for developing new models for future urban infrastructure monitoring and management.\n\n## 5. Conclusions\n\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\n\nOur open-source model lays a valuable foundation for developing machine learning-based models as an alternative to hydraulic models, which future research can further optimize. Future research should focus on enhancing the architecture of GNN and applying more interpretability methods to improve both performance and clarity. Model Performance: First, the model should be tested on more real-world WDSs to verify its robustness and explore the relationship between network characteristics and optimal parameter settings. Additionally, more…1712 tokens truncated…ars ago; Crawled: last month; Explainable forecasting frameworkThe GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. ... It retains the advantages of the GRU in time series prediction while deeply analyzing the complex structure of non-Euclidean space graph data through graph convolution operations, achieving efficient utilization of graph data [31].\n### 2.2. Explainable forecasting framework\n\nThe GRU [6] is a highly optimized variant of the recurrent neural network. Its core advantage lies in its unique architectural design. Through the precise regulatory mechanisms of the update gate and reset gate, the GRU skillfully balances the retention and forgetting of historical information [7]. This allows it to capture the dynamics of long-term and short-term dependencies in time series data [24]. The GGNN, an innovative extension of the GRU [17], integrates recurrent layers, graph convolution, and gating mechanisms. It retains the advantages of the GRU in time series prediction while deeply analyzing the complex structure of non-Euclidean space graph data through graph convolution operations, achieving efficient utilization of graph data [31]. However, the GGNN also faces limitations of the convolution-based graph methods: Each iteration only focuses on the first-order neighborhood of the sensor nodes. Thus, the iteration of the recurrent layers restricts the generalization of sensor node features to a larger network space [32].\n\nBuilding upon the dynamic global weighting capability of attention mechanisms, this study enhances the GGNN framework by integrating self-attention and external-attention modules. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. Fig. 1 illustrates the overall AGN architecture. The subsequent sections detail the design and implementation of this framework:\n\nImage: Fig 1\n\n  1. X′ = fGNN( ̃X, E, A; Θ)
(6)
where fGNN : RN×dnode × RN×N × RM×dedge ↦RN×dnode is a generic GNN function that takes partial‐observable
feature matrix ̃X, the topology A, and edge attributes E as inputs and yields reconstructed features X′ charac-
terized by model weights Θ. The key idea is to find the optimal weights that satisfy the minimum error between
predicted and ground truth nodal features. Mathematically, the objective is formalized as follows:
Θ∗= argmin

Figure 4. Graph neural network (GNN) training scheme. Given clean snapshots, we mask out a significant number (95%)
of node features. The remaining data is then sent into a GNN playing as an autoencoder to rebuild missing values with regard
to graph properties (such as topology and edge attributes). GNN weights are updated using the loss derived by the predicted
and ground truth values at masked places.

Empirically, we use the mean square error (MSE) as a default loss function L for GATRes because it yields the
best result in our tests. In addition, inspired by BERT (Devlin et al., 2018), the loss is computed on masked
positions. After computation, model weights Θ are updated by the partial derivatives w.r.t the computed loss. For
detail, we refer to gradient descent optimization techniques (Kingma & Ba, 2014; Ruder, 2016). The training
progress is then iterated with different masked features ̃X derived from the original features X until the model
convergence.

### aggregated_output

```text
cal uncertainty is used here to describe the variability of the
topological graph of the network due to a pipe valve with un-
known status (open/closed). This can also be considered as op-
erational uncertainty because, typically, valves change status
during operations, such as repairs, that have taken place in
the network.
Sensors and Telemetry
We assume that there is one tank water level sensor, a total of three
flow sensors, one at the pump and one at each of the DMA entran-
ces, and 33 pressure sensors, all transmitting their measurements
every 5 min to the utility’s SCADA system. There are no time de-
lays in the data transmission and no lost packages. Pressure sensors
give an average value of the last 5 min, which mitigates the uncer-
tainty due to pressure transients in the system. In addition, 82
AMRs were installed in Area C for delivering water consumption
data directly to the SCADA system. Each AMR gives the aggre-
gated consumption of many users in the AMR area.
The locations of the pressure sensors are depicted in Fig. 3, and
the AMRs in Fig. 4. Sensor readings do not have errors nor time
delays. The simulated sensor readings are rounded to two decimal
points; in practice this reduces the amount of data sent over the
telecommunications network.
Leakage Modeling
We assume that the only faults affecting the system during the
2-year operation are background leakages and pipe bursts. Any
preexisting leakages in the network are assumed to be small rel-

    # Retrieve the nodal pressures
    nominal_pressure = nominal_wdn_model.get_simulated_pressure()
    
    x,y,scale,bias = dataCleaner(pressure_df    = nominal_pressure, # Pass the nodal pressures
                                 observed_nodes = sensors,          # Indicate which nodes have sensors
                                 rescale        = scaling,          # Perform scaling on the timeseries data
                                 mode           = mode,             # Set data curation mode (n_timesteps / sensor_mask)
                                 task           = task,             # Set data curation task (prediction  / reconstruction)
                                 n_timesteps    = n_timesteps)      # Perform scaling on the timeseries data
    
    # Split the data into training and validation sets
    x_trn, x_val, y_trn, y_val = train_test_split(x, y, 
                                                  test_size    = 0.2,
                                                  random_state = 1,
                                                  shuffle      = False)
    
    '''
     3. Get Historical Data
    '''
    print('3. Retrieving historical data \n')
    
    # Load the data into a numpy array with format matching the GraphConvWat problem
    N = len(nodes)
    T = scada_data.pressures.shape[0]
    P = scada_data.pressures[nodes].values
    V = scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].values
    
    K0 = np.zeros((N,N))
    K1 = np.zeros((N,N))
    Kd = np.zeros((N,N))
    
    ref_nodes = []
    for i,node in enumerate(nodes):
        ref_node = Ref_node(node)
        models=[]

        X_tr = np.concatenate([scada_data.pressures[node].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1),
                               scada_data.flows['PUMP_1'].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)],
                             axis=1)

        for j,node_cor in enumerate(nodes):
            y_tr = scada_data.pressures[node_cor].loc[cor_time_frame[0]:cor_time_frame[1]].to_numpy().reshape(-1,1)
            model = LinearRegression()
            models.append(model.fit(X_tr, y_tr))
            K0[i,j] = model.intercept_[0]
            K1[i,j] = model.coef_[0][0]
            Kd[i,j] = model.coef_[0][1]
        ref_node.set_models(models)
    
    np.fill_diagonal(K0,0)
    np.fill_diagonal(K1,1)
    np.fill_diagonal(Kd,0)
    
    e = np.multiply.outer(K0,np.ones(T))\
        + np.multiply.outer(Kd,V)\
        + np.multiply.outer(K1,np.ones(T))*np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(1,2,0)\
        - np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(2,1,0)
    
    if 'n215' in nodes:
        e[nodes.index('n215'),:,:] *= 0.02
    
    res = np.zeros((N,T))
    for t in range(T):
        e_t_i = e[:,:,t].copy()
        e_t_i[e_t_i<0]=0
        e_t_i[e_t_i>0]=1
        i_ = pd.Series(e_t_i.sum(axis=0)).sort_values(ascending=False).index.to_list()
        i_max = int(i_[0])
        
        res[i_max,t] = np.linalg.norm(e[:,:,t][:,i_max])
        
    MRE = pd.DataFrame(res.T,index=scada_data.pressures.index,columns=nodes)
    return MRE

np.multiply.outer(P,np.ones(N)).transpose(2,1,0)\\r\\n    \\r\\n    if 'n215' in nodes:\\r\\n        e[nodes.index('n215'),:,:] *= 0.02\\r\\n    \\r\\n    res = np.zeros((N,T))\\r\\n    for t in range(T):\\r\\n        e_t_i = e[:,:,t].copy()\\r\\n        e_t_i[e_t_i<0]=0\\r\\n        e_t_i[e_t_i>0]=1\\r\\n        i_ = pd.Series(e_t_i.sum(axis=0)).sort_values(ascending=False).index.to_list()\\r\\n        i_max = int(i_[0])\\r\\n        \\r\\n        res[i_max,t] = np.linalg.norm(e[:,:,t][:,i_max])\\r\\n        \\r\\n    MRE = pd.DataFrame(res.T,index=scada_data.pressures.index,columns=nodes)\\r\\n    return MRE\\r\\n    \\r\\ndef leak_analysis_individual(scada_data,node_MAS,closest_nodes,time_frame_list):\\r\\n    states = []\\r\\n    for time_frame in time_frame_list:\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\"}"

```text
La fuente oficial aporta una precisión importante: las presiones son promedios de los cinco minutos anteriores, están redondeadas a dos decimales y el benchmark no simula errores de sensor, retrasos ni pérdida de paquetes. Lo haré explícito para definir bien el objetivo y los límites de aplicación.

También detecté un ajuste en la prueba secundaria: el modelo base debe recibir los mismos datos históricos que se usan para calcular el balance del tanque. Así podremos evaluar el efecto de la transformación sin darle información adicional a una sola variante.
```
